Notwithstanding the 1978 and 1982 amendments, one widely held opinion remains that the protections afforded to listed species curtail economic activity.[^cite_note-150] In the extreme, economic consequences can induce [perverse incentives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perverse_incentives) by which landowners actively curtail their lands from attracting endangered species. An example in the eastern USA pertains to the endangered [red-cockaded woodpecker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red-cockaded_woodpecker).[^cite_note-red-woodpecker-fws-151] A 1995 study of some 1,000 privately owned forest plots within the range of the woodpecker found that when landowners observed pine growth maturing to a stage in which it might attract nesting woodpeckers, they were more likely to harvest – regardless of timber prices at the time.[^cite_note-\:22-152] This is a form of intentional [habitat destruction](1707053.md) for avoiding economic consequences. By 2024, however, recovery actions had gone well enough for the federal agency to downlist the species from endangered to threatened.[^cite_note-red-cockaded-press-153][^cite_note-Lufkin-2024-154] Downlisting was achieved in large part because of a linked pair of innovative conservation documents negotiated between landowners and the federal agency: **Conservation Benefit Agreement (CBA)** and **Enhancement of Survival (EOS)** permit.[^cite_note-red-cockaded-press-153][^cite_note-fws-cba-155][^cite_note-fws-eos-156]

Legislators have expressed that the ESA has been "weaponized," particularly against western states, constraining state government choices about the use of public lands.[^cite_note-western-157] The case of the protracted dispute over the [greater sage-grouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_sage-grouse) is one such example,[^cite_note-NYT-2010-158][^cite_note-CBD-2024-159] and the [spotted owl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spotted_owl) is another.[^cite_note-boyt-160] In the extreme is the largely western saying pertaining to endangered animals, such as wolves: "[shoot, shovel, and shut up](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shooting,_shoveling,_and_shutting_up)."[^cite_note-ESA-2016-5][^cite_note-bailey-161][^cite_note-penn-2023-86] Rep. [Don Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Young) (Alaska), the longest-serving Republican congressman, said in 2018, "As the one person in the Congress, the only one, that voted for the Endangered Species Act, please beat me with a whip."[^cite_note-162]

Some economists have stated that finding a way to reduce such perverse incentives would lead to more effective protection of endangered species.[^cite_note-163] One suggestion for ending perverse incentives would be to compensate property owners for protecting endangered species, rather than having an endangered species regarded as a potential financial loss.[^cite_note-bailey-161] Professor [Jonathan Adler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_H._Adler) of [Case Western Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_Western_Reserve_University_School_of_Law) has argued that requiring compensation for the costs of ESA land-use controls could make the Act more effective, because compensation:[^cite_note-\:2-164]

1. would signal the accurate cost of land-use regulation, which would in turn lower the government's "overconsumption" of such regulation,

2. would foster more efficient land development,

3. would facilitate production of more reliable species data (because landowners would be more open to having scientists conducting research on their property), and,

4. would give landowners a reason to maintain their property in species-friendly condition.

   [^cite_note-\:2-164]

As well, while the standard to prevent jeopardy or adverse modification, under Section 7 of the ESA, applies only to federal activities (which can, however, include private activities that require a federal permit), entirely non-federal activities are subject to Section 10 of the Act.[^cite_note-165] To work toward conservation solutions before perverse incentives develop, the "Conservation Without Conflict" initiative and coalition was formed in 2017, with guidance presented in a series of eight steps.[^cite_note-conservation-conflict-166] Other collaborations supported by nongovernmental organizations focus on ranching and forestry stewardship initiatives.[^cite_note-2025-schwartz-118]

### The biocentric focus of listing and recovery

A law review article published in 2024 states, "Alone among major environmental laws, the ESA explicitly prioritizes the protection and conservation of non-human species and constrains the ability of government agencies to consider trade-offs."[^cite_note-2024-Tarnished-Gold-29] It was at the highest judicial level that the [biocentric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biocentrism_\(ethics\)) interpretation of the ESA was declared in 1978. The Supreme Court ruling in *[Tennessee Valley Authority v. Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee_Valley_Authority_v._Hill)* stated, "The plain intent of Congress in enacting this statute was to halt and reverse the trend toward species extinction, whatever the cost."[^cite_note-2024-Tarnished-Gold-29] In this case, the cost was the halting of dam construction underway on the [Little Tennessee River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Tennessee_River), based on the listing of a small freshwater fish, the [snail darter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snail_darter) in 1975. *[See also 'Snail darter controversy'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snail_darter_controversy)*.

The [Congressional Research Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Research_Service), in a report titled "The Endangered Species Act: Overview and Implementation," surveys the language and history of this elevation of biocentrism in federal law:

> The ESA expressly requires the Services to make listing determinations "*solely* on the basis of the best scientific and commercial data available." Congress added the word *solely* in the 1982 amendments to the ESA to clarify that the determination of endangered or threatened status was intended to be made without reference to its potential economic impacts. In discussing the addition of the word *solely*, a committee report stated the word is "intended to remove from the process of the listing or delisting of species any factor not related to the biological status of the species." The committee further stated that it "strongly believes that economic considerations have no relevance to determinations regarding the \[listing] status of species," and that applying economic criteria to the listing process is prohibited by the inclusion of the word *solely* in the legislation.[^cite_note-CRS-ESA-2021-2]



[Tiehm's buckwheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eriogonum_tiehmii) in Nevada.

[^cite_note-ecos-buckwheat-167]

Ever since the Supreme Court ruling in the snail darter case, interest groups with a biocentric focus have had strong grounding for challenging agency listing decisions. As reported by the Congressional Research Service:

> Citizen suits frequently have been used to compel agency action and direct agency resources under the ESA. ESA citizen suits have been used to compel the Services to list, reclassify, or delist species; challenge delays in listing decisions; oppose listing, reclassification, or delisting rules; address critical habitat designations and revisions; and challenge BiOps \[Biological Opinions] and use of the Section 7 consultation process. A subset of citizen suits has addressed deadlines under the ESA (i.e., *deadline suits*). The [Government Accountability Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Accountability_Office) reported that most deadline suits from 2005 to 2015 were related to the Services missing deadlines on petitions to list species under the ESA. The study found that most of the suits were resolved through settlements that established timelines for completing the listing process.[^cite_note-CRS-ESA-2021-2]

Another consequence of the [snail darter ruling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snail_darter_controversy) is that formal listing decisions sometimes include lengthy expositions of scientific details. This way, it is clear that the "best available science" has indeed been consulted. A highly controversial, and thus lengthy, listing decision was issued in 2022. It pertains to a small flowering plant in the desert of southwest [Nevada](21216.md).[^cite_note-CBD-buckwheat-2022-168] The published document in the *[Federal Register](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Register)* that establishes [Tiehm's buckwheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiehm's_buckwheat) as an endangered species is 33 pages long, inclusive of a "critical habitat" designation for 910 acres that was simultaneously issued.[^cite_note-FR-buckwheat-2022-final-169] Six of the total pages are responses to each of 29 comments elicited when the required draft of the listing was published. The foundational document that established the "best available science" for making the listing decision was previously published as a "**Species Status Assessment**", which in itself was 80 pages long in PDF format.[^cite_note-2022-buckwheat-SSA-170]

### Accommodating other national priorities



[Tiehm's buckwheat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eriogonum_tiehmii) in the [lithium](17561.md)-rich landscape of southern Nevada

A recent example of competing priorities happened when the federal government was compelled to make a listing decision on a petition. In 2019 the [Center for Biological Diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Biological_Diversity) petitioned to have a small flowering plant in Nevada [^cite_note-CBD-buckwheat-2022-168] listed as endangered.[^cite_note-CBD-buckwheat-2022-168] A mining project by [Ioneer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ioneer), an Australian company, was already preparing the site for extracting [lithium](17561.md) and [boron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boron). Environmental interests were thus divided between those focused on species protection and those advocating for electric vehicles, which require lithium as the key component of the lightest-weight batteries.[^cite_note-2021-Wired-buckwheat-171]

While economic concerns and priorities are restricted from playing a role in governmental listing decisions, the 1978 amendments to the Act did make it possible for these matters to impinge on the designation of critical habitat.[^cite_note-FWS-amendments-ESA-28]

&#x20;Because the endangered listing decision and critical habitat specification occurred in the same document in 2022, this resulted in something unusual: Both the Center for Biological Diversity (CBD) and the mining company posted support for the decision. CBD was enthusiastic about the endangered listing. Ioneer already had developed a plan to begin mining that would not interfere with the 910 acres of critical habitat. But CBD was skeptical:

> In Ioneer's latest operations plan,[^cite_note-2022-Ioneer-buckwheat-protection-plan-172] which covers the first phase of the mine, it proposes avoiding a tiny island of land containing 75% of the buckwheat population. The island would be surrounded by an open pit mine and tailings dumps within just 12 feet of the rare wildflowers. Ioneer falsely claims this will conserve the buckwheat.... "Ioneer's 'Buckwheat Island' scenario would spell doom for this sensitive little flower," said Donnelly. "Now that the buckwheat is protected, we'll use the full power of the Endangered Species Act to ensure Ioneer doesn't harm one hair on a buckwheat's head."[^cite_note-CBD-buckwheat-2022-168]



Approval for the mine, 2024

[^cite_note-2024-buckwheat-BLM-approve-173]

In this case, the critical habitat and its surrounds were entirely on public lands managed by the [U.S. Bureau of Land Management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Bureau_of_Land_Management). It thus fell to that agency, in consultation with the Fish and Wildlife Service, to make the final decisions about whether, where, and how the mine could proceed.

The Bureau granted approval October 2024. Numerous associated documents can be found on the agency's webpage for the Rhyolite Ridge Project,[^cite_note-2024-buckwheat-BLM-approve-173] with all the underlying reports and documents fulfilling the requirements of the [National Environmental Policy Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Environmental_Policy_Act).[^cite_note-BLM-buckwheat-NEPA-174] The approval was preceded by a 2023 "Public Scoping Report" that was 15 pages, plus more than 100 pages of appendices.[^cite_note-BLM-buckwheat-NEPA-174]

Ioneer also has a publicly accessible website, where it publishes all the documents preceding and related to the permit decision.[^cite_note-Ioneer-permitting-175] One of the links goes to "Ioneer's Buckwheat Protection Plan Summary: Proposed Conservation Measures for Tiehm's Buckwheat and Critical Habitat."[^cite_note-Ioneer-plan-summary-176] Another page on the website describes the company's past and future conservation efforts. Two botanists have been hired to work in the greenhouse to conduct research "to develop protection measures that will ensure the plant can thrive in natural conditions, including the extremes posed by climate change."[^cite_note-Ioneer-conservation-177]

### Effectiveness



[American alligator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_alligator), delisted 1987

[^cite_note-Defenders-2023-178]

The 50th anniversary of the ESA was in December 2023. As the time approached, interest groups and journalists began writing about the results of its implementation. As of August that year, an aggregate of 1,780 species had been cumulatively listed as "**endangered**" or a lesser category of "**threatened**". While 99% of the total species were still alive, critics pointed out that only 64 species had improved enough to be declared as **recovered** and thus removed from the list (**delisted**).[^cite_note-AP_2023-08-04-179] A data point on the positive side was that a different set of 64 species had improved enough to be **downlisted** from endangered to threatened.[^cite_note-reclassified-180]

Among the most celebrated successes is that of the [American alligator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_alligator). This reptile was listed as endangered in 1967. Twenty years later it was delisted as fully recovered.[^cite_note-Defenders-2023-178] However, because of its resemblance to its endangered saltwater relative in Florida, the [American crocodile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_crocodile), hunting and commercial trade regulations have been maintained for this species.[^cite_note-alligator-2008-181]





[Bachman's warbler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachman's_warbler) was declared extinct, but [Ivory-billed woodpecker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivory-billed_woodpecker) was not.

The most controversial measure of effectiveness, however, pertained to the published number of cumulatively listed species that had gone **extinct**. As the autumn of 2023 began, the official number was 11.[^cite_note-AP_2023-08-04-179] But another 23 species had gone missing for so long that in September 2021 the agency had published a 41-page draft ruling in the *Federal Register* to officially declare those 23 species extinct.[^cite_note-FR-extinct-2021-182][^cite_note-2021-extinct-doi-183]

It took two years for the agency to finalize its proposed ruling, but in October 2023 the controversy was settled. The agency ruled as extinct 21 of the 23 species it had proposed. Declared extinct were [Bachman's warbler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bachman's_warbler), which had been native to [Florida](18933066.md) and [South Carolina](27956.md), along with nine tropical birds (8 in [Hawaii](13270.md) and 1 in [Guam](11974.md)). Hope still remained for one bird, the [Ivory-billed woodpecker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivory-billed_woodpecker). The only other species that remained listed as endangered was a Hawaiian plant in the mint family. Hope for recovery thus continued for the Hawaiian variety of *[Phyllostegia glabra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phyllostegia_glabra)* "due to new surveys identifying new, potentially suitable habitats for the species."[^cite_note-21-delisted-184][^cite_note-2023-extinct-final-185]

Another controversy has been ongoing for more than three decades. Some have argued that the recovery of imperiled flesh-eating birds (notably, the [bald eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bald_eagle), [brown pelican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_pelican), and [peregrine falcon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peregrine_falcon)) should be attributed to the 1972 ban of the pesticide [DDT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DDT) by the [EPA](58666.md), rather than the Endangered Species Act.[^cite_note-capital-press-139] Supporters of the Act argue that listing these species as endangered led to additional actions that were also crucial for species recovery (i.e., captive breeding, habitat protection, and protection from disturbance).[^cite_note-\:3-186][^cite_note-187][^cite_note-falcon-2024-188]



Examples of freshwater [mussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mussel) imperiled in the eastern USA

Among the most difficult species to protect are [mussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mussel) because they depend on adequate amounts of clean and flowing freshwater. Home to approximately 300 mussel species, the eastern region of the USA is the center of global diversity for these freshwater [molluscs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molluscs). However, 65 percent of them are threatened or endangered.[^cite_note-mussel_2023-189]

The 1988 Congressional amendments to the Act[^cite_note-FWS-amendments-190] included a new section, Section 18, to aid effectiveness evaluations by having each of the two implementing agencies periodically report cumulative federal funding (and, to some degree, state funding) on a species-by-species basis.[^cite_note-section-18-191] As of 2026, the agency webpage titled "Endangered and Threatened Species Expenditures Reports" lists and links annual reports from fiscal year 1989 through 2020, but for no later years.[^cite_note-fws-expenditures-webpage-192][^cite_note-Frederico-193] A synthesis report to Congress for fiscal years 2017 through 2020 is also available and provides these statistics: "Of the 1,388 status reviews completed, 93 percent (1,294) recommend no change in status for the species, 3 percent (40) recommend reclassifying from endangered to threatened, 3 percent (38) recommend delisting (22 due to extinction, 13 due to recovery, and 3 due to error), 1 percent (13) recommend reclassifying from threatened to endangered, and less than 1 percent (2) recommend a revision to the listed entity."[^cite_note-USFWS-2020-194]

### Recovery and delistings are rare

The above section on "effectiveness" of the Act is examined here as to why it is that recovery, and thus delistings, are rare.



[Leatherback sea turtle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leatherback_sea_turtle) returning to the ocean after laying eggs in sand pits it had dug in the upper beach

A widely used statistic supporting effectiveness of the Act is that 99 percent of listed species have not gone extinct.[^cite_note-CBD-ESA-50-85][^cite_note-195] In 2012 the [Center for Biological Diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Biological_Diversity) issued a report that surveyed a sample of 110 listed species and concluded that 90 percent of them were recovering "at the rate specified by their federal recovery plan."[^cite_note-196]



As of 2026, the [Hawaiian monk seal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_monk_seal) is still listed as endangered under the ESA.

[^cite_note-monk-seal-197]

Full recovery may thus be slow, but substantial progress is evidenced when a species is **downlisted** from endangered to threatened. For example, [National Marine Fisheries Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Marine_Fisheries_Service) lists eight species (or populations of a species) as among the most at risk of extinction in the near future. These animals are the [Atlantic salmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_salmon); the Central California Coast [coho salmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coho_salmon); the Cook Inlet [beluga whale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beluga_whale); the [Hawaiian monk seal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_monk_seal); the Pacific [leatherback sea turtle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leatherback_sea_turtle); the Sacramento River winter-run [chinook salmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinook_salmon); the southern resident population of [killer whale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orca); and the [white abalone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haliotis_sorenseni). Human activities are presented as the primary cause of extinction threats for all these species. While the two implementing agencies have a combined record of changing species status from threatened to endangered on nine occasions, the number of status improvements from endangered to threatened is greater.[^cite_note-198]

On the opposing side of the spectrum, a foundation associated with the Western Caucus of U.S. senators and representatives issued a 116-page report in 2023 that points to data and statements made by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service during the past half-century that can be interpreted as disputing proclamations of success.[^cite_note-gordon-2023-84] Specifically, statements of numbers of species "recovered" do not distinguish between those delisted owing to actual improvement in populations versus those for which the original population numbers were later found to have been greatly underestimated. Had the science been more in line with reality at the start, this report claims 36 of the 62 species reported by the agency as officially recovered would not have achieved listing at the outset.[^cite_note-gordon-2023-84]



The [Northern flying squirrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_flying_squirrel) entails 25 subspecies in North America.

[^cite_note-fws-flying-squirrel-199]

**Delisting** in 2008 of the [Virginia northern flying squirrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glaucomys_sabrinus) is one of the examples officially counted as a "recovery." However, the basis for delisting proved to be a combination of a wrongly inferred subspecies taxonomic status and a faulty initial population count that neglected to search for the animal across the border into West Virginia.[^cite_note-gordon-2023-84][^cite_note-200]

Controversy also develops when the science used to support a delisting decision differs from the numerical population thresholds included in the **species recovery plan**. For example, the listing of the [California sea otter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_otter) has been controversial with fishermen who contend that the sea otter's expansion from Monterey into Southern California waters has resulted in a dramatic reduction in [sea urchin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_urchin), [abalone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abalone), and other commercially valuable species. These impacts led certain fishermen groups to petition the government to delist the sea otter. The petition argued that delisting is appropriate because, among other reasons, the sea otter's population goals, as contained in the most recent recovery plan, have been met. The government, however, denied the petition.[^cite_note-201] A 2012 court case upheld the agency decision, ruling that published recovery criteria are not legally binding for later delisting decisions.[^cite_note-ESA-2016-5] Accordingly, during the early 2020s an even more controversial agency response against a delisting petition was supported by that court decision. It pertained to the grizzly bear in six distinct populations.[^cite_note-Wired-grizzly-2025-202]

In 2022 a research paper pointed to a set of three factors that contribute to the low number of delistings: "The combination of delays in listing rare species, the typically small population sizes of species at time of listing, and inadequate funding for recovery actions, are the key factors that can explain the relatively small number of listed species that have fully recovered."[^cite_note-Eberhard-2022-131] More fundamentally, while the statute requires the agencies to develop recovery plans, "the ESA imposes no duty to implement recovery plans or to ensure the recovery of listed species."[^cite_note-Berchiolli-2020-203]

In 2024 a "new metric to evaluate recovery progress" was published by 13 coauthors. They noted widespread deficiencies in monitoring population changes and threats through time that hampered the agency in "making recovery actions more achievable and proactive, rather than having conservation efforts remain reactive."[^cite_note-204] A review paper published in 2025 offers that, back when the Act was written, "it was likely unanticipated that many endangered species would have no hope of recovery to the point of not requiring ongoing management attention."[^cite_note-2025-schwartz-118] Therefore, "the law does not provide guidance on managing species for which no recovery option exists."[^cite_note-2025-schwartz-118]

### Delays in specifying recovery actions

Listing of a species "triggers two overlapping types of conservation measures: extinction prevention and recovery actions."[^cite_note-ESA-2016-5] An official document required by the Act has come to be known as a recovery plan. The Act "gives few guidelines for their preparation and content and does not specify a deadline for how soon after listing the Services must complete recovery plans."[^cite_note-ESA-2016-5] A 2023 report on the Act issued by [Defenders of Wildlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defenders_of_Wildlife) calculated that "265 species listed under the Act lack recovery guidance of any kind, while 370 additional species lack final recovery guidance." The group also noted that more than half of the existing recovery plans were more than 20 years old.[^cite_note-Defenders-2023-178]

Besides alleged funding inadequacies,[^cite_note-ESA-2016-5][^cite_note-Defenders-2023-178][^cite_note-CRS-2026-ESA-Appropriations-205] several inherent problems have been pointed to for delays in agency translation of scientific findings into actions beyond extinction prevention and thus actively toward species recovery. One such problem is the "knowledge—action boundary" that distinguishes conservation scientists from conservation managers. That is, how can scientific scholarship be made actionable, and thereby contribute directly to forward-moving policies and practices?[^cite_note-cook-2013-206] Impediments to generating boundary-spanning [conservation science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_biology) include "a reward structure in science that promotes publication and grant income rather than engaging with conservation practitioners."[^cite_note-cook-2013-206] Distrust across the boundary may also develop if conservation managers perceive that ESA funding allocated to research or monitoring reduces what is available for recovery actions.[^cite_note-cook-2013-206]

"Fear of failure in conservation" is another factor that contributes to agency and manager hesitancy to undertake recovery actions for which there is no certainty of success.[^cite_note-meek-2015-207] There are two reasons why scientists themselves may abstain from recommending actions. One is fear that making such recommendations may compromise their status as objective researchers whose conclusions can be trusted. A second is fear that a recommended action that is undertaken but then fails may injure their reputation. These fears may be heightened when recommendations pertain to a species for which controversy has developed or one whose population has declined so drastically that any manipulation of the species or its habitat may later be denounced as contributing to its further decline or extinction.[^cite_note-meek-2015-207] Thus there are multiple reasons why recovery programs for some species "may be trapped in a cycle where more resources are allocated to information gathering versus action."[^cite_note-buxton-2020-208]

Species recovery is slowed by inherent complexities. The U.S. Fish & Wildlife Service maintains a webpage that sets forth the **stages of the recovery process**.[^cite_note-fws-recovery-process-209] Included is a linked list of 7 named "services" that the agency offers to assist collaborative parties in accomplishing beneficial actions themselves:

- recovery challenge grants

- recovery land acquisition grants

- 3-200-59: Scientific Purposes, Enhancement of Propagation, or Survival Permits (Recovery Permits)

- Conservation Benefit Agreements

- 3-200-54: Enhancement of Survival Permits Associated with Conservation Benefit Agreements

- Habitat Conservation Plans

- 3-200-56: Incidental Take Permits Associated with a Habitat Conservation Plan

  [^cite_note-fws-recovery-process-209]

### Failure to implement recovery actions



3-part framework for "Recovery Planning and Implementation" (USF\&WS, April 2019)

[^cite_note-3-part-framework-2019-210]



"Species Status Assessment Framework" of the U.S. Fish & Wildlife Service

[^cite_note-SSA-211]

The U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service itself has recognized that specifying in a written plan the actions to achieve recovery goals falls short of ensuring that such actions will take place.[^cite_note-recovery-planning-212] Indeed, the agency recognizes that "Recovery plans are guidance and not regulatory documents, and no agency or entity is required by the Endangered Species Act (ESA) to implement actions in a recovery plan."[^cite_note-recovery-213] Therefore, beginning in 2016, the agency augmented standard recovery planning by shifting to a three-part framework. Statutory and regulatory requirements for producing an initial **recovery plan** are still fulfilled, including opportunities for public comments. The plan is to be written and published in the customary way, and it continues to be subject to the "five year review" process. However, two new kinds of working documents can be developed, posted, and updated as needed for each listed species.[^cite_note-recovery-planning-212]

The first updatable document is a **Species Status Assessment (SSA)**. It is "a biological risk assessment to aid decision makers who must use the best available scientific information to make policy decisions under the ESA." As new papers are published and rigorous data collected, the SSA can be updated and reposted as warranted.[^cite_note-SSA-211] As of April 2026, 455 listed or petitioned species have had an SSA completed and posted on the FWS aggregate list.[^cite_note-SSA-list-136]

&#x20;SSA updates may, in turn, stimulate changes in the third part of the new framework: the **Recovery Implementation Strategy (RIS)**. While recovery actions are policy level decisions that will continue to be delineated in the official recovery plan, the RIS serves as "a short-term, more flexible operational document focused on how, when, and with whom the recovery actions will be implemented."[^cite_note-recovery-planning-212] A single species may ultimately have a number of RIS documents posted — each pertaining to a different entity, such as a land trust or botanical garden, that steps forward (with or without federal funding) to voluntarily engage in one or more specific recovery actions:

> &#x20;The RIS is to be developed with conservation partners outside of the agency, and focuses on the period of time and scope of activities that work best for those collaborators to achieve recovery goals. Many RISs can be developed, specific to partners and/or activities, and can cover varying timeframes, as needed. If multiple RISs are developed, an 'umbrella framework' (an overarching RIS) outlining the relationships and priorities among the individual RISs is developed to ensure strategic implementation of the overall recovery program for the species.[^cite_note-recovery-planning-212]

Because the agency had been criticized for lacking "clear distinctions between science input and policy application," four FWS agency scientists along with two colleagues in the [U.S. Geological Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Geological_Survey) published a paper in 2018 in the *Journal of Fish and Wildlife Management*. Titled, "Development of a Species Status Assessment Process for Decisions under the U.S. Endangered Species Act," the authors determined that "the SSA results in a scientific report distinct from policy application, which contributes to streamlined, transparent, and consistent decision-making and allows for greater technical participation by experts outside of the USFWS, for example, by state natural resource agencies."[^cite_note-Smith-2018-214]

### Stakeholder initiatives (with or without listing)

With or without listing, there are opportunities for stakeholders themselves to begin actions on their own. This especially applies to habitat improvements. If suitable habitat for a species can be found on private, state, municipal, or tribal lands, there is no need to wait for the federal agency to offer or specify in-place actions. The parties can expand and improve such habitats on their own. For example, a [National Park Service](161535.md) report in 2023 in behalf of the endangered [Karner blue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karner_blue) butterfly included a recommendation to "encourage entities (city, state, county, tribal, federal) with sandy soils north of the current range to plant *Lupinus perennis* and nectar plants" in anticipation of future authorization for "[managed relocation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assisted_migration)" of populations threatened by heat and drought in southerly portions of the butterfly's historical range.[^cite_note-nps-2023-215]

While there are legal limitations on what citizens and other stakeholders can do directly for listed animal species, the field is wide open for obtaining horticulturally produced seeds and specimens of listed plants and then conducting plantings on their own.[^cite_note-shirey-55] A well-known example is a network of citizens who call themselves [Torreya Guardians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torreya_Guardians). [Florida Torreya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torreya_taxifolia) is a [glacial relict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glacial_relict) species of subcanopy tree that was listed as endangered in 1984. Since 2004, the citizen group has been using seeds from mature plantings on private lands in [North Carolina](21650.md)[^cite_note-TG-seeds-216] (and since 2018 also from their own plantings in [Ohio](22199.md)[^cite_note-TG-Ohio-217]) to engage in a form of [assisted migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assisted_migration) poleward of this climate-endangered plant.[^cite_note-theconversation.com-56]



Historically native range of the [Whitebark Pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitebark_Pine)

Prior to listing of a species, stakeholders who wish to act in its behalf have freedom to engage with not only habitat but with the species itself. A well-known example entails actions taken in behalf of the subalpine [Whitebark Pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinus_albicaulis) of the northern [Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains) soon after vast stands suddenly began to die in [Yellowstone National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellowstone_National_Park) and elsewhere. A petition for listing was submitted in 2008, but status as "threatened" was not conferred until 2022. By then, collaboration among concerned parties was well underway without agency direction.[^cite_note-yale-2024-218] Supportive research and actions were undertaken by the conservation organization [American Forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Forests) as well as a new organization specific to the tree: the Whitebark Pine Ecosystem Foundation.[^cite_note-219] Other collaborators include research scientists within the [U.S. Forest Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Forest_Service), geneticists at the [University of California, Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_California,_Davis), and the [Confederated Salish and Kootenai Tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederated_Salish_and_Kootenai_Tribes). The [U.S. Bureau of Land Management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Bureau_of_Land_Management) and the [National Park Service](161535.md) were also involved in consultation prior to listing by the agency in charge of endangered species: the [U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Fish_and_Wildlife_Service).[^cite_note-yale-2024-218] A 188-page **Species Status Assessment** published in 2021 aggregates the scientific information on which the policy decision to list the species as threatened was based.[^cite_note-SSA-whitebark-220]

Controversy is also apparent, though rare, when stakeholders entail activists who regard the "political-economic regime" not as the source of solutions but as "the root cause of biodiversity loss."[^cite_note-mermet-2020-221] In such instances [guerrilla rewilding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rewilding_\(conservation_biology\)) of rare species has been known to occur.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223]

### Collaborative planning on nonfederal lands

The 1982 amendments to Section 10 of the Act authorized collaborative engagement of the implementing agencies with landowners in producing [Habitat Conservation Plans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habitat_Conservation_Plan). Such a plan is a required part of an application for an [Incidental Take Permit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incidental_Take_Permit), issued under the Act to private and other nonfederal entities undertaking projects that might result in harm to a listed species.[^cite_note-HCP-FWS-33] The intent is to deter controversy by building into the plan practices for minimizing harm to listed species and their core habitat needs (including seasonal peaks in use). A validated plan then absolves the landowner (or developer) from harms that may incidentally occur to the species, when following the plan. Securing landowner pledges of habitat enhancement measures during the planning process can serve to defuse opposition from the public and other stakeholders.[^cite_note-HCP-224][^cite_note-HCP-Coalition-225]



[Piping plover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piping_plover) in its Atlantic beach nesting habitat

Plan preparation is generally initiated and produced by a nonfederal entity: private landowner, state government, local government. As of 2021, more than 700 habitat conservation plans were in force for listed species.[^cite_note-Carter-2021-226] A leading example is the plan produced by the state of [Massachusetts](1645518.md) in 2016 to reduce conflicts between a type of beach-nesting shorebird, [piping plover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piping_plover), and recreational beach users along the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md).[^cite_note-HCP-Coalition-225][^cite_note-Mass-2016-227] Such plans also apply to the [red-cockaded woodpecker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red-cockaded_woodpecker) of forests in the southeastern states.[^cite_note-HCN-1997-228][^cite_note-\:22-152] In 2023, habitat conservation planning for [grizzly bears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grizzly_bear) in the northern [Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains) became controversial when an increase in bear deaths caused by trains was reported, along with acknowledgment by the agency that several million dollars of federal funds for the plan participants had not yet been provided for them to engage in their specified mitigation actions.[^cite_note-griz-2023-229]

Summing up the practical difficulties in a 2023 report on the Act, [Defenders of Wildlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defenders_of_Wildlife) pointed to underfunding as a continuing problem and that the agencies "lack adequate resources to develop, approve, and monitor these plans, and there are significant data gaps in how many of these plans are performing."[^cite_note-Defenders-2023-178]

In 2024 a new program went into effect: **Conservation Benefits Agreement** (CBA). It combined and streamlined two previous opportunities for landowners to initiate their own conservation projects and thereby obtain assurances that stabilize how federal policies apply to their actions. The two previous programs pertaining to Section 10(a) of the Act that were consolidated:&#x20;

- **Safe Harbor Agreement (SHA)**: These agreements were for species already listed as threatened or endangered.

- **Candidate Conservation Agreement with Assurances** (CCAA): These were for species that were not yet listed but were possible candidates for future listing.

  [^cite_note-CCA-2024-230]

An example of a CBA that was approved in 2025 was initiated by a minerals corporation with lands in southeastern [Arizona](21883824.md). The voluntary agreement benefits three endangered fish species ([spikedace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spikedace), [loach minnow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loach_minnow), and [Gila chub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gila_chub)) and one threatened snake ([narrow-headed garter snake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrow-headed_garter_snake)). The agreement will remain in effect for 50 years.[^cite_note-CBA-AZ-231]

### Petitions awaiting listing decisions

A 2019 report found that the Fish and Wildlife Service faced a backlog of more than 500 species that have been determined to potentially warrant protection.[^cite_note-PS-2019-232] A 2022 report pointed to severe population declines while species await listing decisions as a major problem impeding eventual recovery success.[^cite_note-Wildlife_Society_2022-233] The decision to list or defer listing of a petitioned species is supposed to take no more than 2 years after a petition is filed.[^cite_note-CRS-ESA-2021-2] However, on average it takes the agency 12 years to finalize a decision.[^cite_note-Puckett_etal2016-234] An analysis published in 2016 by the [Ecological Society of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_Society_of_America) found that approximately 50 species may have gone extinct while awaiting a listing decision.[^cite_note-ESA-2016-5] Additional funding might enable the agency to direct more resources toward biological assessments of petitioned species and determine if they merit a listing decision.[^cite_note-MJ-2023-235][^cite_note-236][^cite_note-CRS-ESA-2021-2]

An additional issue is that species still listed under the Act may already be extinct. For example, the [IUCN Red List](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IUCN_Red_List) declared the [Scioto madtom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scioto_madtom) extinct in 2013. This freshwater species had last been seen alive in its small native range in [Ohio](22199.md) in 1957.[^cite_note-237] However, it was not until 2023 that the FWS officially declared this catfish as extinct.[^cite_note-21-delisted-184]

As of February 2025, the Fish and Wildlife Service online system that catalogs all current listing proposals that have not yet been processed shows there are 65 outstanding domestic and foreign species.[^cite_note-FWS-ESA-proposd-238] The list aggregates the proposed species into broad taxonomic types. Counting only the domestic (not the foreign) species on the list, there are: 5 amphibians, 3 birds, 13 freshwater clams, 3 crustaceans (crayfish), 8 fishes, 3 flowering plants, 7 insects, 2 mammals (1 rat and 1 bat), 12 reptiles, and 2 snails.[^cite_note-FWS-ESA-proposd-238]

### Taxonomic status and hybridization

Bipartisan support for the Endangered Species Act of 1973 began to unravel almost immediately when a tiny fish native to Tennessee, the [snail darter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snail_darter), tested the strong [biocentric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biocentrism_\(ethics\)) focus of the Act. Elsewhere on this page, and also on a distinct wikipedia page, the [snail darter controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snail_darter_controversy) is dealt with at length. Here it is important to mention that in 2025, tools for making taxonomic determinations had advanced to the point that the snail darter is no longer regarded as a species. Rather, it is a geographically distinct population of the [stargazing darter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stargazing_darter) species, which is "present and plentiful in many places in the U.S."[^cite_note-2025-darter-Yirka-239][^cite_note-2025-Ghezelayagh-240][^cite_note-CBD-2025-darter-241]

From the beginning, implementation of the ESA of 1973 is based on "the best available science," beginning with [taxonomic classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxonomy_\(biology\)). The petitioned group of organisms must first qualify as a bona fide [species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Species) or [subspecies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subspecies). Isolated populations or varieties of otherwise common species may be considered for listing only if they are [vertebrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertebrates).[^cite_note-zink-2022-242] Given the [evolutionary processes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution) by which species arise and continue to change, there is no consensus that could provide a singular definition or standard for the agency to use. Classification is therefore determined on a case-by-case basis[^cite_note-Conbio-2016-243][^cite_note-Zaepfel-1995-244] and generally secured by publication in an academic journal — which itself is vulnerable to [publish-or-perish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Publish_or_perish) motivations that can lead to what is called [taxonomic inflation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxonomic_inflation).[^cite_note-Doremus-2010-27] This in itself can make the posting of a listing decision vulnerable to public attack.[^cite_note-Erwin-2017-245] Overturn of the [Chevron deference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chevron_deference) by the U.S. Supreme Court in 2024[^cite_note-2024-Supreme-Court-119] may have amplified classification as grounds for litigation by opponents of endangered species listings.[^cite_note-Chevron-2024-113]



The calyx of the [Gierisch mallow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphaeralcea_gierischii) (left) is smooth compared to that of the more populous [Rusby's globemallow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphaeralcea_rusbyi).&#x20;



Dusky Seaside Sparrow&#x20;

Classification boundaries that are imprecise because of geographically remote or interpenetrating [hybrid populations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_\(biology\)) pose additional challenges for agency staff attempting to ground listing decisions on "best available science."[^cite_note-Erwin-2017-245][^cite_note-CZ-2015-246][^cite_note-Doremus-2010-27] It is not unusual for listing decisions to become complicated by the presence of hybrids, and the forms of hybridization are diverse. To begin, improvements in [conservation genomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_genomics) established that the already-listed [red wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_wolf) evolved as a hybrid of native [gray wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gray_wolves) and [coyotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coyotes) who interbred in the eastern states.[^cite_note-red-wolf-2016-247] The problem compounded when the red wolf population managed on a coastal island became populous enough for geographic range experimentation. Individuals released onto the mainland routinely interbred with the far more populous coyotes, and [genetic swamping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_swamping) ensued.[^cite_note-Erwin-2017-245] Other listed animals embroiled in hybridization controversies include the [golden-winged warbler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden-winged_warbler),[^cite_note-warbler-248] [westslope cutthroat trout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westslope_cutthroat_trout), the [wood bison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wood_bison), and the [eastern massasauga rattlesnake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_massasauga_rattlesnake).[^cite_note-Erwin-2017-245]

An example for plants in which a taxonomic decision required listing as a distinct species a [globe mallow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphaeralcea) in Arizona concerns the genus *[Sphaeralcea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphaeralcea)*. A 45-page **Species Status Assessment** for *[Sphaeralcea gierischii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphaeralcea_gierischii)* (Gierisch mallow) published by the agency in 2024 notes that it was classified as a distinct mallow species in 2002 "based on unique morphological features." Accordingly, "because it is a recently described species, we do not know its historical range." A complication for assessing its status is that "coarse estimates of overall plant abundance, gaps in monitoring duration, and interannual variation in plant numbers make it difficult to detect trends in Gierisch mallow abundance." Whether hybridization affects any of its populations is unknown.[^cite_note-mallow-2024-249] A 2014 publication was cited that "found approximately one-third of interspecific crosses between 30 *Sphaeralcea* taxa successful."[^cite_note-Dreher-2014-250]

Only once has hybridization been an intentional recovery action, known as [genetic rescue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_rescue). This was applied to the [Florida panther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_panther) when [cougars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cougars) from Texas were intentionally released into its habitat to counter ongoing decline owing to [inbreeding depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inbreeding_depression).[^cite_note-Erwin-2017-245] This openness to using hybridization to outright save a species contrasted with the agency's decision to deny cross-breeding of the [dusky seaside sparrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dusky_seaside_sparrow) with its sister species: [Scott's seaside sparrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott's_seaside_sparrow).[^cite_note-Zaepfel-1995-244][^cite_note-Doremus-2010-27] The dusky was declared extinct in 1987.[^cite_note-Erwin-2017-245] A 2025 review paper reports that naturally occurring hybridization that is already happening "threatens to swamp" these listed taxa: [northern spotted owl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_spotted_owl), [bull trout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bull_trout), [California tiger salamander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_tiger_salamander), and [red wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_wolf).[^cite_note-2025-schwartz-118]

### Critical habitat

Because habitat loss is regarded as the primary threat to most imperiled species,[^cite_note-ESA-2016-5] Section 4 of the Endangered Species Act of 1973 allowed the [Fish and Wildlife Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_and_Wildlife_Service) (FWS) and [National Marine Fisheries Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Marine_Fisheries_Service) (NMFS) to designate specific areas as protected "[Critical Habitat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critical_habitat)" zones.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGobleScottDavis200689-251] The designation of critical habitat does not affect land ownership. It does not allow the government to take or manage private property, nor to establish a refuge, reserve, preserve, or other conservation area. Critical habitat designation does not allow unauthorized government access to private land. Such designation can, however, severely restrict activities allowable on private lands.[^cite_note-252]

&#x20;In 1978, Congress [amended the law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endangered_Species_Act_Amendments_of_1978) to make critical habitat designation a mandatory requirement for all threatened and endangered species. To offset some of the consequences of this amendment, and to improve flexibility in the ESA's application, the 1978 amendments also added [economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy) into the process of determining such designation.[^cite_note-\:1-148] It reads, "... shall designate critical habitat ... on the basis of the best scientific data available and after taking into consideration the economic impact, and any other impact, of specifying ... area as critical habitat."[^cite_note-ref-253] The congressional report on the 1978 amendment anticipated a potential controversy that might ensue, given that "economic impact" was included in the amendment:

> "... the critical habitat provision is a startling section which is wholly inconsistent with the rest of the legislation. It constitutes a loophole which could readily be abused by any Secretary ... who is vulnerable to political pressure or who is not sympathetic to the basic purposes of the Endangered Species Act."-- House of Representatives Report 95-1625, at 69 (1978)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStanford_Environmental_Law_Society200168-254]

The controversy did arise and thus the 1982 amendment to the Act explicitly prohibited similar economic considerations when determining the status of a species.[^cite_note-CBD-255]

On the other hand, the congressional report for those 1982 amendments also explained that, "the critical habitat designation, with its attendant economic analysis, offers some counter-point to the listing of species without due consideration for the effects on land use and other developmental interests."[^cite_note-256] According to a 2025 review paper, a range of [environmental social sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_social_science) have since contributed significant "potential for finding solutions" beyond the scope of what the biological sciences could alone offer.[^cite_note-2025-schwartz-118]

In designating critical habitat, the needs of open space for individual and population growth, food, water, light or other nutritional requirements, breeding sites, seed germination and dispersal needs, and lack of disturbances are considered.[^cite_note-CBD-255] As itemized within a 2008 review paper on the Endangered Species Act, designation of critical habitat is the first priority for agency action following listing of a species. Next is formulation of a recovery plan.[^cite_note-schwartz-2008-87] Not all recovery plans, however, specify critical habitat. Setting aside this requirement is authorized if the agency head determines that its designation would not be "prudent."[^cite_note-CRS-ESA-2021-2]



Sample of federal grants in 2024 for endangered species habitat support

[^cite_note-2024-grants-257]

Most provisions of the ESA revolve around preventing extinction. Critical habitat is one of the few that focus on recovery. A 2005 paper published in the journal *[BioScience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BioScience)* concluded that species afforded critical habitat are twice as likely to be recovering than are species without critical habitat.[^cite_note-TaylorSuckling2005-258] A 2016 report published by the [Ecological Society of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_Society_of_America) stated that data were inconclusive on this matter because "in practice, the Services often exempt habitat degradation from regulation. As a result, designating critical habitat has had limited regulatory effect."[^cite_note-ESA-2016-5] Overall, disagreement as to the effectiveness of critical habitat designation is expressed in a number of different reports.[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-CBD-255][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStanford_Environmental_Law_Society200186-260]

Another controversy arises from the Act specifying that critical habitat designation is required to contain "all areas essential to the conservation" of the imperiled species, and may include private as well as public lands. The Fish and Wildlife Service has a policy limiting designation to lands and waters within the U.S., and both federal agencies may exclude essential areas if they determine that economic or other costs exceed the benefit. The ESA, however, is mute about how such costs and benefits are to be determined,[^cite_note-ESA-2016-5] and USFWS and NMFS have frequently minimized this exclusion provision's application in favor of large critical habitat designations.[^cite_note-\:1-148]

All federal agencies are prohibited from authorizing, funding, or carrying out actions that "destroy or adversely modify" critical habitats (Section 7(a) (2)). While the regulatory aspect of critical habitat does not apply directly to private and other non-federal landowners, large-scale development, logging, and mining projects on private and state land typically require one or more federal permits and thus become subject to critical habitat regulations. Outside or in parallel with regulatory processes, critical habitats also focus and encourage voluntary actions such as land purchases, grant making, restoration, and establishment of [reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nature_reserve).[^cite_note-SucklingTaylor2006-261]

The Act's specification of the timing of critical habitat designations has also become problematic. The ESA requires that critical habitat be designated at the time of or within one year of a species being placed on the endangered list. In practice, most designations occur several years after listing.[^cite_note-SucklingTaylor2006-261] Between 1978 and 1986, the FWS regularly designated critical habitat. In 1986 the [Reagan administration](25433.md) issued a [regulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulation) limiting the protective status of critical habitat. As a result, few critical habitats were designated between 1986 and the late 1990s. In the late 1990s and early 2000s, a series of [court orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_order) invalidated the Reagan regulations and forced the FWS and NMFS to designate several hundred critical habitats, especially in [Hawaii](13270.md), [California](5407.md), and other western states. Midwest and eastern states received less critical habitat, primarily on rivers and coastlines. As of December 2006, the Reagan regulation had not yet been replaced, though its use had been suspended. Nonetheless, the agencies have generally changed course. Since about 2005, both have tried to designate critical habitat at or near the time of listing.[^cite_note-SucklingTaylor2006-261]



[Dusky gopher frog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dusky_gopher_frog)

The designation of critical habitat has proved to be one of the most controversial aspects of the ESA's administration. An example of such controversy can be found in *[Weyerhaeuser Company v. United States Fish and Wildlife Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weyerhaeuser_Company_v._United_States_Fish_and_Wildlife_Service).* In this 2018 ruling, the U.S. Supreme Court addressed whether land can be designed as critical habitat even if the land cannot support the existence of protected species. The litigation arose over the designation of [loblolly pine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinus_taeda) timberlands in [Louisiana](18130.md) as critical habitat for the [dusky gopher frog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dusky_gopher_frog). The plaintiffs objected to the designation of their land as critical habitat, citing its significant economic costs, including the inability to develop the land for commercial and residential uses. Unanimously ruling for the property owners, the Supreme Court held that land cannot be designated as critical habitat if it cannot qualify as habitat. As [Chief Justice Roberts](1928850.md)' opinion for the Court explains, "\[a]ccording to the ordinary understanding of how adjectives work, 'critical habitat' must also be 'habitat.' Thus, "\[o]nly the 'habitat' of the endangered species is eligible for designation as critical habitat."[^cite_note-262]

The dusky gopher frog decision at the level of U.S. Supreme Court thus became a key species-specific example of the judiciary setting geographic boundaries on the agency's ability to designate critical habitat happened in 2018. The court ruled as invalid the critical habitat designation of a segment of private timberlands in [Louisiana](18130.md) in part because the endangered frog had been extirpated from that state for half a century, currently inhabiting only [Mississippi](16949861.md). Equally, it was clear that suitable pond "habitat" for the frog would require a degree of ecological restoration to be performed on the private land.[^cite_note-frog-2018-263][^cite_note-frog-2021-264]

A 2020 law review article concluded that critical habitat designations were still far underutilized. A delay in the timing of designations (after listing and thus concurrent with the development of a species recovery plan) would automatically provide "significant amounts of information absent from the species listing stage."[^cite_note-Berchiolli-2020-203]

### Climate change as an endangerment cause

&#x20;In 2005 the [Center for Biological Diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Biological_Diversity) petitioned to list the [polar bear](24408.md) as threatened, with critical habitat in Alaska.[^cite_note-FR-polar-bear-2006-265] [Climate change](5042951.md) was among the identified threats.[^cite_note-Wentz_2022-266] In 2008 the Fish and Wildlife Service issued its decision to list the bear as threatened, while stating that climate change was not amenable to prohibiting or solving within the provisions of the Act:

> 
>
> *Ursus maritimus*
>
> &#x20;Without the requirement of a causal connection between the action under consultation and effects to species, literally every agency action that contributes greenhouse gases to the atmosphere would arguably result in consultation with respect to every listed species or critical habitat that may be affected by climate change. There is currently no way to determine how the emissions from a specific project under consultation both influence climate change and then subsequently affect specific listed species or critical habitat, including polar bears.[^cite_note-FR-polar-bear-2008-267]



Populations of chinook salmon protected as of 2024

In 2023 another large carnivore, the [wolverine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolverine), was added to the list "due primarily to the ongoing and increasing impacts of climate change and associated habitat degradation and fragmentation." The word "climate" appears 135 times in its official posting, which entails 46 pages in the *[Federal Register](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Register)*.[^cite_note-FR-wolverine-2023-268] Rising temperatures and declining snowpack in the contiguous United States (not Alaska) had made cave denning difficult for the remaining populations in the [Cascade Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cascade_Range) and [Rocky Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountains).[^cite_note-wolverine-2023-269] The following year, the southernmost [subspecies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subspecies) of an alpine and tundra bird, the [white-tailed ptarmigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-tailed_ptarmigan), along the west coast of North America was listed as threatened in Washington state because: "The Service has determined that the loss and degradation of its habitat resulting from climate change will endanger the bird in the foreseeable future."[^cite_note-U.S._Fish_and_Wildlife_Service-270][^cite_note-wdfw\.wa.gov-271]

Because distinct populations of [vertebrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertebrate) species and subspecies can be listed, nine river systems where [chinook salmon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinook_salmon) spawn are now protected under the Act (see image at right).[^cite_note-noaa-salmon-272] Salmon spawn in freshwater but mature in marine waters, so both the [Fish and Wildlife Service](75289.md) and the [National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration](37876.md)[^cite_note-noaa-salmon-272] manage the listing and recovery actions.

### Climate adaptation as a recovery tool

While [climate change](5042951.md) was established in 2008 as a legitimate reason for listing a species, subspecies, or population,[^cite_note-Ruhl-2008-273] [climate adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_adaptation) became an option for recovery actions only in 2023. This was when the geographic scope of the regulation pertaining to "**experimental populations**" was amended.[^cite_note-FWS-10j-2023-274][^cite_note-2023-CRS-ESA-exp-pop-32] Although the Endangered Species Act of 1973 (as amended in 1982)[^cite_note-CRS-ESA-2021-2] did not in itself limit the placement of experimental populations to the "**historical range**" of a plant or animal,[^cite_note-section-10-275] a regulatory change in 1984 made prospective translocations beyond the native range more difficult to justify.[^cite_note-Science-2023-276][^cite_note-Ruhl-2008-273]



[Whooping Crane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whooping_Crane) experimental population authorized in 2001.

[^cite_note-FWS-whooping-crane-277]



The seeds of [Relict trillium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trillium_reliquum) are dispersed by ants, hindering its ability to track climate change.

[^cite_note-relict-trillium-278]

Two [Congressional Research Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Research_Service) reports focus on experimental populations. One summarizes its early use for reasons other than climate change,[^cite_note-2023-CRS-ESA-exp-pop-32] and the other focuses on its past and present use in helping the [gray wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gray_wolf) expand much farther into what had been its vast historical range.[^cite_note-2023-CRS-ESA-exp-pop-wolf-279] As of February 2025, 64 species (including several populations of a single species) were listed as already having authorizations to implement "experimental populations." All can proceed on a "non-essential" basis (meaning, losses are expected but will not threaten the integrity of the [gene pool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_pool)).[^cite_note-ecos-experimental-populations-280] The greatest number of authorizations apply to aquatic species (fishes, clams, mussels). Two large and charismatic birds have been placed into locales far outside of where their last remnant populations survived prior to listing as endangered. These are the [California condor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_condor)[^cite_note-ECOS-condor-281] and the [Whooping crane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whooping_crane).[^cite_note-ECOS-crane-282]

In June 2022, the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service published a proposed rule in the *[Federal Register](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Register)* that would "revise section 10(j) regulations under the ESA to better facilitate recovery by allowing for the introduction of listed species to suitable habitats outside of their historical ranges. The proposed change would help improve the conservation and recovery of imperiled ESA-listed species in the coming decades, as growing impacts from [climate change](5042951.md) and [invasive species](67554.md) cause habitats within their historical ranges to shift and become unsuitable."[^cite_note-283] The comment period ended August 2022, with more than 500 comments posted online by supporters and opponents.[^cite_note-284]

As reported on the news page of *[Audubon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audubon)*, adoption of the proposed regulatory change would authorize, for the first time, use of a controversial climate adaptation tool: [assisted migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assisted_migration).[^cite_note-285] [The U.S. Department of Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_the_Interior) on June 30, 2023, announced its decision to modify the section 10(j) "experimental populations" rule generally as proposed a year earlier.[^cite_note-sierra-2023-286] The press release summarized the reason for the change as, "At the time the original 10(j) regulations were established, the potential impact of climate change on species and their habitats was not fully realized, yet in the decades since have become even more dramatic. These revisions will help prevent extinctions and support the recovery of imperiled species by allowing the Service and our partners to implement proactive, conservation-based species introductions to reduce the impacts of climate change and other threats such as invasive species."[^cite_note-FWS-10j-2023-274]



[Karner blue butterfly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karner_blue)



[Torreya Guardians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torreya_Guardians) Video #38

[^cite_note-Torreya-2024-287]

The rulemaking action includes a section summarizing 25 topics entailed in comments submitted in 2022, along with the agency's official response to each.[^cite_note-288] Pre-existing requirements pertaining to experimental populations in the [Code of Federal Regulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code_of_Federal_Regulations) (CFR) still apply. Plans to use "Subpart H: experimental populations" for the purpose of [climate change adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_adaptation) thus require public notice and ultimate placement in the "Species-specific rules" subpart of the CFR regulations of the precise geographic and other details.[^cite_note-CFR-experimental-289] Six months after this [climate adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_adaptation) regulation for listed species was published, the [National Park Service](161535.md) issued a 154-page report on recommendations for halting the decline of the endangered [Karner blue butterfly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karner_blue) in its small and scattered populations across the northeastern states. Among the recommendations were "[managed relocation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Managed_relocation)" of populations from the southern parts of its range into northward habitats with suitable conditions and supportive plants. The report justified the managed relocation action by noting that stakeholders and managers "are growing more supportive of novel science-based interventions to save rare species from climate change–driven extirpation."[^cite_note-nps-2023-215]

Two weeks after the [2024 United States presidential election](59568546.md), the founder of a citizen group, [Torreya Guardians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torreya_Guardians),[^cite_note-TG-history-290] urged the new administration to utilize the experimental populations regulation to recruit more citizens to help endangered plants.[^cite_note-Torreya-2024-287] The group had been using their own resources to do this for a critically endangered tree since 2008.[^cite_note-sierra-2023-286] Utilizing an exception in the ESA just for plants,[^cite_note-291] they had been experimentally planting **Florida torreya**, *[Torreya taxifolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torreya_taxifolia)*, hundreds of miles northward of its native range (a [glacial refugium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glacial_refugium)) in Florida.[^cite_note-Nature-torreya-2017-292] As reported in *Nature Climate Change* journal in 2008,

> At least on a limited scale, it seems that assisted migration is already happening. One of the most well-known cases is the transfer of *Torreya taxifolia*, a Florida conifer with a tiny range that many believe survives there only due to historical accident. In a desperate bid to protect their beloved species, a group of botanists and environmentalists who call themselves the **Torreya Guardians** have established a volunteer seed-planting campaign to move it northwards. On 3 August, they planted 31 *Torreya taxifolia* seedlings in North Carolina.[^cite_note-293]

### When delisting leads to recreational hunting



Gray wolf in North America

In October 2020, the [Gray wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf) (but not the [Mexican wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_wolf)) was delisted by the federal government in all states. Press releases by the FWS explained the scope and rationale of that action.[^cite_note-2020-delist-a-294][^cite_note-2020-delist-b-295] Several lawsuits challenging the delisting were filed in the U.S. District Court for the Northern District of California.[^cite_note-FR-2023-296] A press release issued by one of the plaintiffs, [Defenders of Wildlife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defenders_of_Wildlife), explained, "This decision made wolves vulnerable to hunting, trapping, poisoning, and other lethal controls. Without federal protections, state laws threaten to undo decades of public restoration efforts and prevent the goal of true, nationwide wolf recovery."[^cite_note-defenders-court-297] The advocacy group later posted a history of the status and protection of the gray wolf in the lower 48 states, beginning with its endangered listing in 1978, followed by the first reintroduction of the species into [Yellowstone National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellowstone_National_Park) in 1993.[^cite_note-298]

November 2020, the [Congressional Research Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Research_Service) published a 46-page report on the gray wolf listing history, subtitled, "**A Case Study in Listing and Delisting Challenges**."[^cite_note-2020-CRS-ESA-wolf-111] A summary paragraph states the challenges:

> From initial listing to recovery and reintroduction efforts to more recent attempts to delist the gray wolf, FWS has addressed such issues as uncertainties in gray wolf taxonomy, ambiguous statutory terms (e.g., "foreseeable future" and "significant portion of its range"), and the adequacy of state management plans. Stakeholders have questioned FWS's choices in comments to the proposed rules and have challenged many of the agency's gray wolf rules in court. Many of the legal challenges to FWS's delisting rules have succeeded, with courts vacating the rules and remanding them to the agency. The history of FWS's regulation of the gray wolf under the ESA and related litigation serve as a useful case study in how regulatory and legal challenges have shaped FWS's interpretation and application of key terms when listing and delisting species under the Act.[^cite_note-2020-CRS-ESA-wolf-111]

&#x20;The challenges continued after the report was published.[^cite_note-2023-CRS-ESA-exp-pop-wolf-279] As reported in *The Guardian* (March 3, 2021):

> &#x20;Hunters and trappers in [Wisconsin](33127.md) killed 216 gray wolves last week during the state's 2021 wolf hunting season – more than 82% above the authorities' stated quota, sparking uproar among animal-lovers and conservationists, according to reports. The kills all took place in less than 60 hours, quickly exceeding Wisconsin's statewide stated limit of 119 animals. As a result, Wisconsin's department of natural resources ended the season, which was scheduled to span one week, four days early.[^cite_note-2021-Guardian-299]

In February 2022, the district court vacated and remanded the 2020 delisting rule. That decision reinstated the federal protections that were in place prior to the effective date of the 2020 delisting rule. Gray wolves were thus once again listed as threatened in [Minnesota](19590.md) and endangered in all or portions of 44 U.S. states and Mexico. The regional exception where delisting was maintained spanned the 5 states where the [Northern Rocky Mountain wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Rocky_Mountain_wolf) population had established.[^cite_note-FR-2023-296]



"Species Status Assessment" for the Gray wolf in 2023.

[^cite_note-2023-SSA-gray-wolf-300]

&#x20;The agency once again went to work on updating the **Species Status Assessment Report**,[^cite_note-2023-SSA-gray-wolf-300] and creating a formal listing document plus a recovery plan. This culminated in its posting in November 2023 in the *Federal Register* a rulemaking titled "**Reinstatement of Endangered Species Act Protections for the Gray Wolf (*Canis Lupus*) in Compliance with Court Order**."[^cite_note-FR-2023-296] More details and justification were set forth in the agency's posting in February 2024 of a Species Status Review for the gray wolf.[^cite_note-fws-nov-2024-a-301][^cite_note-fws-news-302] It simultaneously announced the beginning of a new process for what it called a "National Recovery Plan" and explained:

> &#x20;Debate over the management of wolves has included more than two decades of legislation, litigation and rulemaking. Wolf recovery to date has been construed around specific legal questions or science-driven exercises about predicted wolf population status. Courts have invalidated five out of six rules finalized by the Service on gray wolf status, citing at least in part a failure to consider how delisting any particular population of gray wolves affects their status and recovery nationwide.[^cite_note-fws-nov-2024-a-301]

&#x20;In May 2024, the U.S. House of Representatives passed a bill to delist the wolf that would override any decision by the agency. The Senate did not follow through.[^cite_note-WPR-2024-303] Meanwhile the [Nez Perce Tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nez_Perce) has been cooperating with federal authorities in [wolf reintroduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf_reintroduction) on its own tribal lands in [Idaho](14607.md) since the 1990s. The tribe maintains a webpage titled, "**The Homecoming of Hîmiin: A Long Time Coming**," and thus expresses a welcoming attitude unusual in the [Rocky Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rocky_Mountain) states:

> &#x20;Ultimately, our efforts to restore the gray wolf to the Nez Perce homeland and his rightful place in Nez Perce culture have been a resounding success. Today, many hundreds of wolves live within the Tribe's homeland in Idaho, Washington, and Oregon. As the management of wolves shifts to state agencies, the Tribe continues to track population trends and engage regional partners to ensure the continued health and prosperity of hîmiin in our homeland.[^cite_note-Nez-Perce-wolf-304]

The Nez Perce Tribe is not unusual in its respectful relationship to the wolf.[^cite_note-2021-Guardian-299] The 2023 species status assessment published by the federal government includes an appendix titled, "Indigenous Knowledge and Cultural Significance of the Gray Wolf to Tribal Nations with Lands in Idaho, Montana, and Wyoming."[^cite_note-2023-SSA-gray-wolf-300] [Traditional ecological knowledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_ecological_knowledge) and cultural values of nine tribes are summarized in that appendix.



Grizzly bear management in 6 populations.

[^cite_note-SSA-grizzly-2024-305]

Another large predator species that has been fraught with ESA listing controversy for many years are the populations of [grizzly bear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grizzly_bear) outside of [Alaska](624.md). The grizzly, too, has a "species status assessment" prepared by the federal government. Here, too, the Nez Perce Tribe is reported as prohibiting grizzly hunting on tribal lands.[^cite_note-SSA-grizzly-2024-305] The 398-page federal report does not, however, include any mention of [traditional ecological knowledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditional_ecological_knowledge) pertaining to the bear.
